--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 1:**

The champion (score 7777.62) is dramatically better than the 2nd place (9934.29). Key characteristics of the champion:
- State 0: GoAwayColor (green, clr=2) → transitions to state 3 on seeing green (C=7, l=2)
- State 1: Attraction with many transitions back to state 3
- State 2: Stop, transitions back to state 0 on black floor
- State 3: Stop, transitions back to state 3 on gray floor

The mission: minimize score means robots should be **stationary before switch** and **moving after switch**. The green wall lights up at switch time, so robots should detect green and start moving.

The champion seems to have robots mostly stopped (states 2 and 3 are Stop), transitioning to GoAwayColor when green is detected. This is clever: before switch, stop; after switch (green appears), move away from green wall.

**Strategy:**
1. Keep champion verbatim (elitism)
2. Exploit: small variations of champion topology
3. Explore: cleaner 2-state designs (Stop → move when green detected)

**Key insight:** The optimal behavior is:
- Before switch: all robots stop (score = 0 per stationary robot per step)
- After switch: all robots move (score = 0 per moving robot per step)
- Trigger: green wall appears → start moving

Simple 2-state: Stop → Exploration (when green detected)

Let me design variations:

**Exploitation of champion:**
- Vary probabilities slightly
- Keep the core structure (Stop states + GoAwayColor triggered by green)

**Exploration:**
- Simple 2-state: Stop → Exploration on C=7 (green)
- 2-state: Stop → GoAwayColor on green detection
- 3-state with cleaner logic

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 2:**

The champion (4932.83) is a 4-state machine. Key structure:
- State 0: GoAwayColor (green, clr=2) → transitions to state 2 (Stop) on seeing green (C=7, p=0.7943) OR to state 2 via random (C=5, p=0.002)
- State 1: Attraction → transitions to state 2 via random/gray/few-neighbors
- State 2: Stop → transitions to state 0 on black floor (C=0, p=0.9411)
- State 3: Stop → transitions to state 2 on gray floor (C=2, p=0.44)

Wait, let me re-read. State 3 transitions to state 2 (since with 4 states, from state 3, destination index 2 maps to... the others are [0,1,2], so D=2 = state 2). And state 0 transitions to destination index 2 = state 2 as well (others from state 0 are [1,2,3], so D=2 = state 3... wait).

Actually with 4 states, from state 0, the "other states" are [1,2,3], indexed as D=0→1, D=1→2, D=2→3. So `--n0x0 2` from state 0 means destination D=2 = state 3, and `--n0x1 2` also = state 3.

From state 1: others are [0,2,3], D=0→0, D=1→2, D=2→3. All `--n1x* 2` means → state 3.

From state 2: others are [0,1,3], D=0→0. `--n2x0 0` → state 0.

From state 3: others are [0,1,2], D=2→2. `--n3x0 2` → state 2.

So the flow is:
- State 0 (GoAwayColor-green): → State 3 (Stop) when green seen (p=0.79) or randomly (p=0.002)
- State 1 (Attraction): → State 3 (Stop) via multiple conditions
- State 2 (Stop): → State 0 (GoAwayColor) on black floor
- State 3 (Stop): → State 2 (Stop) on gray floor

Interesting! So robots mostly cycle between states 2 and 3 (both Stop), triggered by floor color. State 0 moves them away from green. The key insight is: most robots are stopped, and when green appears, they move away from it.

The champion improved dramatically from 7777 to 4932 by adding the `--n0x1 2` random transition (p=0.002) from state 0 to state 3.

**The top 3 machines share the same 4-state topology.** Let me focus on exploiting this.

Key parameters to vary:
- `--p0x0` (probability of transitioning to Stop when seeing green): 0.7943 in champion
- `--p0x1` (random fallback from state 0): 0.002
- `--p2x0` (transition from Stop to GoAwayColor on black floor): 0.9411
- `--p3x0` (transition between Stop states on gray): 0.44
- `--att1` and state 1 transitions

The 2nd and 3rd place machines have 1 transition from state 0 (not 2) - this seems slightly worse. The champion's addition of the small random transition (0.002) from state 0 is key.

**Strategy:**
1. Keep champion verbatim
2. Exploit: vary probabilities around champion values
3. Explore: try removing state 1 (Attraction) which seems less useful, or using Exploration instead

Let me think more carefully. The mission wants robots STOPPED before switch, MOVING after switch. Green light = switch signal. So:
- Before switch: be stopped
- After switch (green visible): start moving

The champion has GoAwayColor-green as state 0, which moves robots. State 2/3 are Stop. The robot moves away from green after seeing it, then goes back to stop. This seems suboptimal - ideally robots should stay stopped until green appears, then move continuously.

But score 4932 is good. Let me try more direct designs too.

For exploitation, I'll vary:
- p0x0: try 0.9, 0.95, 0.6
- p0x1: try 0.001, 0.005, 0.01
- p2x0: try 0.8, 0.95, 1.0
- p3x0: try 0.3, 0.5, 0.6
- att1: try 1.5, 2.5

For exploration:
- Try a cleaner 3-state: Stop → GoAwayColor on green, GoAwayColor → Stop on no-green
- Try Stop-only with green trigger to Exploration

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 3:**

The top 6 machines are all scoring around 5136-5167, very close to the champion (4932.83). The topology is consistent: 4-state machine with:
- State 0: GoAwayColor (green) → Stop (state 3) when green seen + tiny random fallback
- State 1: Attraction or Stop → various transitions to Stop (state 3)
- State 2: Stop → GoAwayColor (state 0) on black floor
- State 3: Stop → Stop (state 2) on gray floor

Key observations:
1. The champion has `p0x0=0.7943, p0x1=0.002` - slightly lower green-detection probability is actually better than 0.9 or 0.95
2. `p2x0=0.9411` (transition from stop to GoAwayColor on black floor) seems important
3. `p3x0=0.44` seems important (transition between stop states)
4. State 1 seems almost irrelevant - machines with Stop in state 1 (rank 6) score same as with Attraction
5. The 3-state version (rank 9, score 6103) is worse - 4 states helps

**Key insight:** The two stop states (2 and 3) with a GoAwayColor state create a mechanism where:
- Robots mostly stay stopped
- When they see green (after switch), they move to stop state 3
- State 2 can reactivate GoAwayColor via black floor condition

The p0x1=0.002 random escape from GoAwayColor to stop is critical - it shouldn't be too high (0.01 was bad, rank 12) or too low.

**What to try:**
- Fine-tune p0x0 around 0.7943 (try 0.6, 0.65, 0.7, 0.75)
- Fine-tune p0x1 around 0.002 (try 0.001, 0.0015, 0.003)
- Fine-tune p2x0 around 0.9411 (try 0.85, 0.9, 1.0)
- Fine-tune p3x0 around 0.44 (try 0.3, 0.35, 0.5, 0.55, 0.6)
- Try different state 1 behaviors
- Try adding green-detection transition to state 2 (stop→goawaycolor when green seen)

The fact that machines with Stop in state 1 score same as Attraction suggests state 1 is rarely visited or irrelevant. Let me try making state 1 also trigger GoAwayColor directly.

Also: what if state 2 (Stop) also transitions to GoAwayColor when it sees green? That would make the switch faster.

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 4:**

The scores are very tightly clustered around 5020-5036 for ranks 2-10, with the champion at 4932.83. The topology is extremely stable - all top machines use the same 4-state structure. 

Key findings:
1. `p0x1=0.0015` (rank 1, 4967) vs `p0x1=0.002` (champion, 4932) - the champion's 0.002 is better
2. `p0x1=0.001` (rank 11, 5174) - too low is worse
3. `p0x1=0.003` (rank 12, 5348) - too high is worse  
4. `p0x0=0.7943` consistently appears in best machines
5. `p3x0` seems insensitive (0.3, 0.44, 0.55 all score ~5020)
6. `p2x0` seems insensitive (0.85, 0.9411, 1.0 all score ~5020)
7. Adding a second green-detection transition to state 3 (rank 12, 13) makes things worse
8. State 1 content barely matters - Attraction and Stop both score similarly

The scores are converging but still ~90 points above champion. The champion's exact parameter combination (p0x0=0.7943, p0x1=0.002) seems hard to beat with small tweaks.

**I need to explore more broadly.** The current topology has a fundamental structure:
- State 0: GoAwayColor(green) - moves after green detected
- States 2,3: Stop - keeps robots still before switch

The stochastic evaluation means scores vary run-to-run. The champion might just have gotten lucky. Let me try:
1. Keeping champion (elitism)
2. Fine-tune p0x0 more carefully (try 0.8, 0.85, 0.9 combined with p0x1=0.002)
3. Try p0x1 around 0.0025, 0.002
4. Try completely different topologies - what if we use GoToColor(green) to move TOWARD green after switch? That also causes movement.
5. What if state 2 transitions to state 0 on seeing green (not black floor)?
6. Try exploration behavior instead of GoAwayColor

The floor is uniformly GRAY, so condition C=0 (black floor) would rarely fire... wait, maybe the arena has black walls? Or the floor near walls is darker? Actually the condition fires based on floor sensor, and the floor is gray. So C=0 (black floor) would fire with some probability on gray floor.

Actually wait - C=0 fires when floor is BLACK. C=1 fires when floor is GRAY. If floor is uniformly gray, then C=0 should rarely fire. But state 2's transition `--c2x0 0 --p2x0 0.9411` says "transition to state 0 when floor is BLACK with p=0.9411". If floor is always gray, this would never fire... unless there are dark spots near walls.

Hmm, but the machines work well. Maybe the robots do reach darker areas near walls? Or maybe the floor color sensors have noise?

Actually, maybe I'm wrong about the floor. Let me re-read: "The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY." So C=0 (black) would be very rare, meaning state 2 rarely transitions to state 0. State 3 transitions to state 2 on gray (p=0.44) - this fires frequently since floor is gray.

So the flow is mostly: Start in state 0 → see green → go to state 3 (Stop) → gray floor → go to state 2 (Stop) → rarely go to state 0.

Actually this means robots quickly settle into Stop states 2 and 3, cycling between them. State 0 (GoAwayColor) is rarely visited after the initial period. The "see green" triggers moving robots to stop states faster. Before the green appears, robots in state 0 move (bad before switch), but there's a tiny p=0.002 random escape to stop.

Wait - but shouldn't robots START in state 0 (GoAwayColor)? Initially there's no green, so they don't see it. They'll stay in state 0 moving around until either they randomly escape (p=0.002/step) or they see green.

With p=0.002, expected time to escape state 0 = 500 steps. So robots spend ~500 steps in GoAwayColor before escaping to Stop. That's bad! Unless... the random escape rate is per step and they escape much faster.

Actually with p=0.002 per step, the expected number of steps in state 0 before escaping is 1/0.002 = 500 steps. But the switch time is 400-600 steps. So some robots may still be in state 0 moving when the switch happens. After the switch (green appears), they see green and transition to state 3 (Stop) with p=0.7943.

Hmm, this seems suboptimal. Maybe I need to try much higher p0x1 (say 0.005-0.01) to get robots into Stop faster before switch, at the cost of them sometimes missing the green signal.

But we saw p0x1=0.01 was bad (rank 12 in round 3, score 8103). And p0x1=0.003 is rank 12 here (5348). So 0.002 is already near-optimal.

Let me think differently. Maybe the key is the initial state should be Stop, not GoAwayColor. If robots start in Stop, they're immediately scoring well before the switch. After switch, they need to see green and start moving.

The issue with starting in Stop is: how do they reach the GoAwayColor state when green appears? We need Stop → GoAwayColor on green detection.

Let me try: State 0 = Stop, transitions to GoAwayColor when green detected. State 1 = GoAwayColor, stays moving. State 2 = Stop (backup).

Also try: What if we use GoToColor instead of GoAwayColor? Moving toward green also scores after switch.

Let me also try varying p0x0 more systematically around 0.7943.

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 5:**

Remarkable: ranks 1-7 ALL score exactly 4931.73 - the same score as the champion (previously 4932.83, now 4931.73 after re-evaluation variance). This means many different parameter variations are essentially equivalent. The topology is very robust.

Key observations:
1. p0x0 can vary widely (0.7943, 0.8) without changing score
2. State 1 content barely matters (Attraction with various params, or Stop all score the same)
3. p2x0 condition type can be C=0 (black) or C=1 (gray) or C=7 (green) - all same score
4. State 3 p3x0 doesn't matter much

The score has essentially plateaued at ~4931. The stochastic noise in evaluation dominates. 

**What could genuinely improve?**
- The champion structure: GoAwayColor → Stop → Stop → Stop with tiny random escape
- The fundamental bottleneck: robots spend time moving in state 0 before escaping to stop

The score of ~4932 out of max 1200*20=24000 suggests robots are stopped about 80% of the time and moving 20%, or some mix. 

**What's theoretically optimal?**
- Before switch (~500 steps): all 20 robots stopped → 0 score contribution
- After switch (~700 steps): all 20 robots moving → 0 score contribution
- Perfect score = 0

Current score ~4932 means roughly 4932/(20) ≈ 247 steps of wrong behavior per robot.

**New ideas to try:**
1. What if we add a direct transition from state 0 to state 0 via green detection with HIGHER probability? No, can't self-transition.
2. What if state 0's random escape probability is tuned more finely - try 0.0015, 0.0018, 0.002, 0.0022?
3. What if we try GoToColor(green) instead of GoAwayColor(green) for state 0? Moving toward green after switch is also moving.
4. What if we try Exploration instead of GoAwayColor?
5. What if we add a transition from state 2 or 3 back to state 0 when green is NOT seen (C=7 with low p)?

Actually, looking more carefully: the `--c2x0 0 --p2x0 0.9411` transition from state 2 (Stop) on BLACK floor fires rarely since floor is GRAY. So state 2 is essentially a sink. State 3 transitions to state 2 on gray floor (p=0.44). 

The flow: robots start in state 0 (GoAwayColor), randomly escape to state 3 (p=0.002/step), then cycle between states 2 and 3 (both Stop). When green appears, state 0 sees it and transitions to state 3 (p=0.7943).

The key is: how fast do robots get from state 0 to a Stop state? With p=0.002, expected ~500 steps. But the switch is at 400-600 steps. So many robots may still be in state 0 when green appears.

**Critical insight**: After the switch, being in state 0 (GoAwayColor-green) means robots MOVE (good after switch). Robots that escaped to Stop states 2/3 need to see green to get back to state 0, but state 2 has C=0 (black) which rarely fires, and state 3 has C=2 (gray) which fires with p=0.44 - but that goes to state 2, not state 0!

So after the switch: robots in Stop states 2/3 stay stopped (BAD). Robots in state 0 see green and go to state 3 (BAD - they stop). 

Wait - this seems suboptimal! After the switch, we WANT robots to move. But the machine pushes all robots to Stop states.

**The real optimal would be**: before switch → stop; after switch → move. The green signal triggers the switch. So we need: Stop until green appears, then move when green is detected.

But the champion keeps STOPPING when green is detected (state 0 → state 3). That seems backwards...

Unless: the robots in state 0 BEFORE the green appears are already moving (bad for pre-switch), but the expected time in state 0 is 500 steps, and switch is at 400-600. So robots escape state 0 quickly enough before the switch... but then they're stopped when green appears (can't move after switch).

I'm confused about why this machine scores well. Let me re-examine.

Actually wait - maybe I'm wrong about the direction. Let me re-read the scoring:
- Before switch: score += 1 per STATIONARY robot (bad to be stationary... wait no)
- "score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary" before switch
- "score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving" after switch

Score is MINIMIZED. So:
- Before switch: want robots MOVING (not stationary)
- After switch: want robots STATIONARY (not moving)

I had it backwards! Let me reconsider.

So the champion should have robots MOVING before switch and STATIONARY after switch. Green appears at switch time.

- State 0 (GoAwayColor-green): robots MOVE. Before switch, no green visible → they stay in state 0 moving (GOOD before switch). After switch, green visible → they transition to state 3 (Stop) (GOOD after switch).
- States 2, 3 (Stop): robots stationary. Bad before switch, good after switch.

But with p=0.002 random escape from state 0 to state 3, some robots prematurely stop before the switch - that's the source of score loss!

So the optimization should:
1. Keep p0x1 (random escape from GoAwayColor to Stop) as LOW as possible
2. Keep p0x0 (transition from GoAwayColor to Stop when green seen) as HIGH as possible  
3. States 2/3 should ideally transition back to GoAwayColor before switch, but they can't know when the switch happens

The problem is: once robots reach states 2/3 (Stop), they're penalized before switch. The black-floor condition C=0 in state 2 is supposed to bring them back to state 0, but floor is gray so it rarely fires.

**Better design ideas:**
1. Make state 2/3 transition back to state 0 on GRAY floor (C=1) with some probability - this would cycle robots back to moving
2. Use C=5 (random) to escape from Stop states back to GoAwayColor
3. Try GoToColor instead of GoAwayColor (moving toward green = moving = good after switch)

**New hypothesis**: The champion works because:
- State 0 (GoAwayColor): robots move, searching. p=0.002 random escape is tiny → robots mostly stay moving
- State 3 (Stop): when green appears, robots stop. But state 3 → state 2 on gray (p=0.44) and state 2 → state 0 on black (rare)

So most robots stay in state 0 (moving) until green appears, then go to Stop. That's the intended behavior!

The score of ~4932 comes from:
- ~20 robots × ~500 steps before switch × p=0.002 = ~20 robots escape per step × some fraction stop early

Actually: each robot in state 0 has p=0.002 of escaping to state 3 per step. Expected steps before escape = 500. Switch at ~500 steps. So about half the robots escape before the switch on average. Each premature stop costs 1 per step.

To reduce score, we want p0x1 as low as possible. But 0.001 was WORSE than 0.002 (rank 11 in round 4). That's strange.

Maybe p0x1=0.002 is needed as a fallback when green is never seen (though green always appears at switch). Or perhaps the simulator requires at least some escape probability.

Actually 0.001 scored 5174 in round 4 vs champion's 4932. So 0.002 is better than 0.001. Puzzling.

Let me try extremely different values: 0.0005, 0.001, 0.0015, 0.002, 0.0025 to map the landscape.

Also try making state 2 transition back to state 0 on gray floor with low probability (so robots that prematurely stopped can re-enter moving state).

And try genuine exploration: what if we use Exploration (B=0) instead of GoAwayColor?

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 6:**

Key findings:
1. Champion stays at 4931.73 - no improvement found yet
2. p0x0=1.0 (rank 1, 5037) - higher green detection is slightly worse than champion's 0.7943
3. p0x0=0.95 with extra green transition (rank 2, 5037) - similar
4. p0x1=0.0005 (rank 11, 5942) - too low is bad
5. p0x1=0.001 (rank 10, 5267) - worse than 0.002
6. p0x1=0.002 remains optimal
7. GoToColor (B=8) instead of GoAwayColor (B=9) scored 5176 (rank 9) - worse
8. The 3-state simplified version (rank 5, 5040) - very close! This is interesting.
9. Extra transitions from state 3 back to state 0 (rank 12, 7712) - bad

**Critical insight from rank 5:** The 3-state machine:
- State 0: GoAwayColor(green) → Stop(state 1) when green seen (p=0.9), random escape to Stop(state 1) (p=0.002)
- State 1: Stop → Stop(state 2) on gray (p=0.44)  [actually → state 1 which is index 1 = destination 1 = state 2 with 3 states from state 1: others are [0,2]]
- State 2: Stop → Stop(state 1) with p=0.001

Wait, let me re-read: `--nstates 3 --s0 9... --n0x0 1... --n0x1 1... --s1 1... --n1x0 1... --s2 1... --n2x0 1`

From state 0: D=1 → state 1 (since others from state 0 are [1,2]? No, with 3 states, from state 0, others are [1,2], D=0→1, D=1→2). So `--n0x0 1 --c0x0 7` and `--n0x1 1 --c0x1 5` both go to state 2!

From state 1: others are [0,2], D=1→2. So `--n1x0 1 --c1x0 2` goes to state 2.
From state 2: others are [0,1], D=1→1. So `--n2x0 1 --c2x0 5` goes to state 1.

So: State 0→State 2 (on green or random), State 1→State 2 (on gray), State 2→State 1 (randomly with p=0.001).

This essentially means: State 0 (moving) → State 2 (stop) when green seen. States 1 and 2 are both stop and cycle with tiny probability. Score 5040 is close but not better.

**What might genuinely help?**
The score ~4932 seems to be a near-optimal floor given the stochastic nature. Let me think about what's actually happening:

Before switch (~500 steps): 20 robots in state 0 (GoAwayColor). Since no green, they stay in state 0 MOVING. Score increases by 0 per step (we want them moving before switch). Wait, I was confused again.

Let me re-re-read: "Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary." So BEFORE switch: stationary is BAD. We want moving.

"From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving." AFTER switch: moving is BAD. We want stationary.

So the machine needs:
- Before switch: robots MOVING (state 0, GoAwayColor with no green visible = keeps moving)
- After switch: green appears → robots STOP (transition from state 0 to stop states)

The p0x1=0.002 random escape from state 0 to stop is actually HARMFUL (stops robots prematurely before switch). But it seems to help - why?

Perhaps: without the random escape, robots in state 0 would never escape even after switch if the green detection fails (robots facing away from wall). The random escape ensures some robots eventually stop even if they can't see green.

But wait - after switch, robots in state 0 see green (with p=0.7943 per step) and go to state 3. The expected time to see green from state 0 is about 1/0.7943 ≈ 1.26 steps. So very fast!

Actually, `--p0x0 0.7943` with C=7 (perceive green color) - this fires with p=0.7943 IF green is perceived. The robot needs to actually perceive the green wall. If it's facing away, it won't perceive it. So detection is not guaranteed every step.

The score ~4932 over 1200 steps × 20 robots = 24000 maximum. Score 4932/24000 ≈ 20.5% "wrong" behavior. This seems like there's still room to improve.

**New ideas:**
1. Try having state 0 with 3 transitions: green detection (high p), green detection (second copy with different p), AND random escape - to catch more green sightings
2. Try GoToColor instead of GoAwayColor for state 0 (both cause movement before switch; GoToColor moves toward green after switch which is still movement = bad after switch... wait no, after switch we want STATIONARY)

Actually GoToColor toward green MOVES robots after switch (bad). GoAwayColor moves them away from green (also bad after switch). We need robots to STOP after switch.

The key is: state 0 transitions to stop states when green detected. Whether state 0 uses GoAwayColor or GoToColor doesn't matter much for the detection logic, but GoAwayColor may be better as it moves robots away from walls (reducing collision/crowding effects).

**Truly new exploration:**
- What if state 0 transitions to a GoAwayColor state (not stop) when green detected, and that state eventually stops? No, GoAwayColor when green detected would keep them moving...

Actually, rethinking: after the switch, robots in state 0 that DON'T see green immediately will still be moving (bad). They need to stop ASAP. What if we add a second condition in state 0 that fires independently?

Let me try: state 0 with transition on "few neighbors" (if robots scatter, neighbor count drops, might indicate post-switch exploration behavior). But this is speculative.

Let me focus on what works and try finer adjustments:
- Try p0x0 = 0.7, 0.75, 0.8 (between 0.7943 and lower values)
- Keep p0x1 = 0.002 (established as optimal)
- Try different p3x0 values (0.1, 0.2, 0.6, 0.8)
- Try making state 2 → state 0 on gray with very low probability (to recover moving robots that got stuck in stop)

Also: what if we try adding a transition from state 0 directly to state 2 (Stop) AND keep the transition to state 3? Let me try having state 0 transition to state 2 OR state 3 on green detection - different stop states.

Let me also try something completely different: what about a simpler machine where state 0 = Stop with green transition to state 1 = GoAwayColor, and state 1 = GoAwayColor with no way back? This would be:
- Before switch: Stay stopped (bad! score accumulates)
- After switch: green detected → start moving (good)

That's actually the WRONG behavior! We want moving BEFORE switch and stopped AFTER. So the champion's approach (moving state 0 by default, stop on green) is correct.

Wait - now I'm second-guessing myself again. Let me be absolutely clear:

Score is MINIMIZED. Before switch: +1 per STATIONARY robot. After switch: +1 per MOVING robot.

So: Before switch → robots should MOVE (to avoid score). After switch → robots should STOP (to avoid score).

Champion: State 0 = GoAwayColor (moves, no green before switch) ✓. After switch: green appears → state 0 detects green → go to Stop state ✓. This is correct behavior!

The residual score (~4932) comes from:
1. Robots that escape state 0 randomly (p=0.002/step) before the switch → they stop early (bad)
2. Robots in state 0 after switch that don't immediately see green (bad)
3. Any time in Stop states before switch

To minimize score:
- p0x1 should be as low as possible (but 0.001 and 0.0005 were worse!)
- p0x0 should be as high as possible (but 1.0 was slightly worse)

The paradox: p0x1=0.001 is WORSE than p0x1=0.002. Why?

Hypothesis: With p0x1=0.001, robots stay in state 0 longer. State 0 is GoAwayColor (green). If there's no green, robots just wander. But GoAwayColor behavior - what does it do when there's no green to avoid? It may exhibit different movement patterns. If GoAwayColor stalls/stops when no target is visible, then robots in state 0 might be stationary (counting against score)!

That would explain everything: GoAwayColor with no visible target = robot stops or moves erratically. The p0x1=0.002 escape ensures robots that aren't finding green move to an explicit Stop state... but wait, Stop also accumulates score before switch.

Actually maybe GoAwayColor with no visible target just continues forward/exploration-like. Let me re-read: "B=9 GoAwayColor -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour". If no object of that color is visible, the behavior might default to some forward motion or stop.

Given the empirical results, p0x1=0.002 seems optimal. Let me accept this and focus on other improvements.

**For round 7, I'll:**
1. Keep champion (elitism)
2. Try variations with multiple green-detection transitions in state 0 (both pointing to stop)
3. Try the 3-state simplified version with tuned parameters
4. Try completely fresh topologies with different state counts
5. Focus on making stop-to-goaway transition work better

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 7:**

New champion: 4928.59! The key change is state 0 now has **4 transitions** instead of 2:
- Two green detection transitions (both C=7, p=0.7943, l=2, destination=state 3)
- Two tiny random escapes (both C=5, p=0.001, destination=state 3)

So the champion has duplicate green detection conditions and duplicate random escapes - effectively making the combined green detection probability higher and random escape ~0.002 combined (two independent p=0.001 transitions).

Wait, actually with two C=7 transitions both firing independently at p=0.7943 each step when green is visible, the combined probability of triggering AT LEAST ONE = 1-(1-0.7943)^2 ≈ 1-0.0423 = 0.9577. And two C=5 at p=0.001 combined ≈ 0.002.

The rank 7 machine (5140) has the same structure but with only 3 transitions (one green + two duplicates), slightly worse. So 4 transitions with two green detections is better.

**Key insight from new champion:**
- Duplicate green detection conditions combine to give ~0.958 effective probability
- Duplicate random escapes combine to ~0.002 total escape rate
- This is slightly better than single p=0.7943 + single p=0.002

**Exploitation ideas:**
1. Try varying the two green detection probabilities independently (e.g., 0.7943 + 0.9, or 0.9 + 0.9)
2. Try varying the two random escape probabilities (0.001 + 0.001, 0.0015 + 0.0015, etc.)
3. Try 4 transitions with different combinations
4. Try keeping the p0x1 transition to a DIFFERENT stop state (state 2 vs state 3)

**Theoretical analysis:**
Combined probability of green detection with two independent C=7 at p1 and p2:
= 1-(1-p1)(1-p2) when green visible

With p1=p2=0.7943: combined = 1-(0.2057)^2 = 1-0.0423 = 0.9577
With p1=p2=0.9: combined = 1-(0.1)^2 = 0.99
With p1=p2=0.95: combined = 1-(0.05)^2 = 0.9975

Let me try higher combined green detection.

Also: what if we have transitions to BOTH state 2 AND state 3 (different stop states)?

State 1 in the champion only has 2 transitions now - let me also try variants with state 1 having more transitions.

**New champion structure analysis:**
From state 0: n0x0→state 3 (C=7, p=0.7943), n0x1→state 3 (C=7, p=0.7943), n0x2→state 3 (C=5, p=0.001), n0x3→state 3 (C=5, p=0.001)

Wait - all four transitions go to state 3? Let me verify: `--n0x0 2` with 4 states from state 0, others=[1,2,3], D=2→state 3. Yes, all 4 go to state 3.

From state 1: `--n1x0 2 --n1x1 2` both → state 3. From state 2: → state 0. From state 3: → state 2.

So the flow is:
- State 0 (GoAwayColor): robots move. → State 3 (Stop) via green detection or random.
- State 1 (Attraction): → State 3 via random or gray floor.
- State 2 (Stop): → State 0 via black floor (rarely fires on gray floor).
- State 3 (Stop): → State 2 via gray floor (p=0.44).

The cycle between states 2 and 3 keeps robots stopped. State 0 is the "moving before switch" state.

**Ideas for improvement:**
1. Have some transitions from state 0 go to state 2 AND some to state 3 (for redundancy)
2. Try higher green detection probabilities in the two C=7 transitions
3. Try 3 green detection + 1 random
4. Try having state 3 also transition back to state 0 occasionally (helps after switch for robots stuck in stop)
   - Wait no: after switch green appears, we want robots in state 0 to STAY moving (but state 0 transitions to stop on green detection). And we want stopped robots to STAY stopped.

Actually after switch: robots in state 0 detect green and stop (good). Robots in stop states want to stay stopped (good). So the current design is already optimal post-switch.

Pre-switch: robots should be in state 0 (moving). The random escape (p=0.002/step) causes premature stopping. Lower p0x2/p0x3 might help.

Let me try p0x2=p0x3=0.0005 (even lower random escape):

5. p0x1=0.7943 is confirmed as optimal for green detection

The champion's key features are firmly established:
- Two green detection transitions at p=0.7943
- Two random escapes at p=0.001 each (combined ~0.002)
- State 1 (Attraction) mostly irrelevant
- State 2 (Stop) with black floor condition
- State 3 (Stop) with gray floor condition

The score improvement from 4931 to 4928 was modest. We seem to be near a local optimum.

**What hasn't been tried yet:**
1. Varying `--att1` in state 1 (currently 1.8228)
2. Varying `--p1x0` and `--p1x1` in state 1 transitions
3. Varying `--p2x0` (currently 0.9411, black floor condition)
4. Varying `--p3x0` (currently 0.44, gray floor condition)
5. Trying different destination states from state 0 (currently all → state 3)
   - What if some transitions go to state 2 instead of state 3?
6. Trying 3 green detection conditions instead of 2
7. Varying the combined random escape rate more carefully

**The paradox of p0x2=0.001 being optimal:**
- p0x2=0.0005 is worse (5512)
- p0x2=0.0015 is worse (5693)  
- p0x2=0.001 is optimal

This is a very tight optimum. Let me try p0x2=0.0008, 0.0012 to see if there's a sweet spot.

**New insight:** What if I try having the two random escapes go to DIFFERENT stop states?
- n0x2 → state 2 (C=5, p=0.001)
- n0x3 → state 3 (C=5, p=0.001)

This distributes robots between stop states differently.

**Also:** What if p3x0 (gray floor transition from state 3→2) is very different? Currently 0.44. If it's very low, robots stay in state 3. If high, they cycle quickly to state 2, which has the rare black-floor trigger back to state 0.

**Strategy for round 9:**
- Keep champion (elitism)
- Try sending some transitions from state 0 to state 2 (not just state 3)
- Try fine-tuning p3x0 (0.1, 0.2, 0.3, 0.5, 0.6)
- Try fine-tuning att1 
- Try p0x2=0.0008, 0.0012
- Try 3 green detection transitions

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
